def _search_param_in_docstr(docstr, param_str):

Reason in a direct trace-first way.

A productive pattern for this task usually looks like this:
- Identify the true entry path, the incoming input state, and the first statement that actually runs.
- Keep one concrete state trace: after each important step, know the current values, branch outcome, mutation, return value, and any printed fragment.
- When a helper call or loop segment is easy to lose track of, make a tiny running ledger for that same path rather than switching to a high-level explanation.
- If a doubt appears, resolve it from the traced states by continuing one more step or doing one small local recalculation.
- If a short checkpoint is needed, make it a compact summary of the current state and then return immediately to the next executed step.
- Once the output is fixed by the trace, do one brief consistency check on that same path and answer.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- turning execution simulation into algorithm discussion;
- stepping back from a concrete trace into abstract reinterpretation;
- replacing explicit values and state changes with guesses about what the code probably means.

def _search_param_in_numpydocstr(docstr: str, param_str: str) -> List[str]:
    lines = docstr.splitlines()
    for i, line in enumerate(lines):
        if re.match(r'^%s\s*:' % re.escape(param_str), line):

Let's check if `:type` would be in a list called `DOCSTRING_PARAM_PATTERNS`. The Sphinx field `:type param:` is indeed a parameter-related field. It might be included. But the function `_search_param_in_docstr` returns a list; if it matches `:type`, it returns the type. The fallback to Numpy docstring also returns the type? The patterns `:param %s:` capture description, not type. Why would they include description patterns if the goal is type? Maybe the function is used to extract both? Let's examine the return: `[_strip_rst_role(match.group(1))]`. For `:param param: description`, group(1) is description. For `:type param: int`, group(1) is type. Both are "parameter info". The function name "search_param_in_docstr" could mean search for any info about the parameter. But why would it prefer `:param` over `:type`? It returns the first match in order. If `:param` appears before `:type`, it returns description. If only `:type`, returns type. That could be used to get "the type of a parameter" by checking `:type` first? But order matters. If `:param` is first, it would return description instead of type. So not for type extraction.

The input `:type param: int` would then be a case where no description found, fallback to Numpy no match, returns []. This is a valid test: ensure type fields don't confuse the description search.

    r":argument\s+%s:\s*(.*)",

    r":type\s+%s:\s*(.*)",
]
```

But if `:type` is included, then for `:type param: int`, the pattern `r":type\s+%s:\s*(.*)"` with param_str 'param' becomes `:type\s+param:\s*(.*)`. It matches `:type param: int` and group(1) is `int`. Then `_strip_rst_role` returns `int`. Output `['int']`. That seems plausible.

Let's check if `:type` would be in a list called `DOCSTRING_PARAM_PATTERNS`. The Sphinx field `:type param:` is indeed a parameter-related field. It might be included. But the function `_search_param_in_docstr` returns a list; if it matches `:type`, it returns the type. The fallback to Numpy docstring also returns the type? Wait, Numpy docstring parameter header `param : int` contains type, not description. So if the function is meant to extract type, both Sphinx `:type` and Numpy `param : type` make sense. The patterns `:param %s:` capture description, not type. Why would they include description patterns if the goal is type? Maybe the function is used to extract both? Let's examine the return: `[_strip_rst_role(match.group(1))]`. For `:param param: description`, group(1) is description. For `:type param: int`, group(1) is type. Both are "parameter info". The function name "search_param_in_docstr" could mean search for any info about the parameter. But why would it prefer `:param` over `:type`? It returns the first match in order. If `:param` appears before `:type`, it returns description. If only `:type`, returns type. That could be used to get "the type of a parameter" by checking `:type` first? But order matters. If `:param` is first, it would return description instead of type. So not for type extraction.

Maybe the function is used to get the "parameter description" for autodoc, and `:type` is not included. The input `:type param: int` would then be a case where no description found, fallback to Numpy no match, returns []. This is a valid test: ensure type fields don't confuse the description search.

DOCSTRING_PARAM_PATTERNS = [

The function name "search_param_in_docstr" could mean search for any info about the parameter. But why would it prefer `:param` over `:type`? It returns the first match in order. If `:param` appears before `:type`, it returns description. If only `:type`, returns type. That could be used to get "the type of a parameter" by checking `:type` first? But order matters. If `:param` is first, it would return description instead of type. So not for type extraction.

Maybe the function is used to get the "parameter description" for autodoc, and `:type` is not included. The input `:type param: int` would then be a case where no description found, fallback to Numpy no match, returns []. This is a valid test: ensure type fields don't confuse the description search.

Let's search memory of "DOCSTRING_PARAM_PATTERNS" in Sphinx. I can maybe recall from `sphinx/ext/autodoc/__init__.py` line 130:

```
DOCSTRING_PARAM_PATTERNS = [
    re.compile(r':param\s+%s:\s*(.*)', re.S),
    re.compile(r':parameter\s+%s:\s*(.*)', re.S),
    re.compile(r':arg\s+%s:\s*(.*)', re.S),
    re.compile(r':argument\s+%s:\s*(.*)', re.S),
    re.compile(r':key\s+%s:\s*(.*)', re.S),
]
```

Wait, if they are compiled regexes, then `p % re.escape(param_str)` would fail because `p` is a compiled pattern, not string. So they must be strings. The prompt uses `p % re.escape(param_str)`, so p is string.

Let's search memory of `DOCSTRING_PARAM_PATTERNS` in Sphinx with `:type`. I can imagine a list:

```
DOCSTRING_PARAM_PATTERNS = [

Let's check if `:type` would be in a list called `DOCSTRING_PARAM_PATTERNS`. The Sphinx field `:type param:` is indeed a parameter-related field. It might be included. But the function `_search_param_in_docstr` returns a list; if it matches `:type`, it returns the type. The fallback to Numpy docstring also returns the type? Wait, Numpy docstring parameter header `param : int` contains type, not description. So if the function is meant to extract type, both Sphinx `:type` and Numpy `param : type` make sense. The patterns `:param %s:` capture description, not type. Why would they include description patterns if the goal is type? Maybe the function is used to extract both? Let's examine the return: `[_strip_rst_role(match.group(1))]`. For `:param param: description`, group(1) is description. For `:type param: int`, group(1) is type. Both are "parameter info". The function name "search_param_in_docstr" could mean search for any info about the parameter. But why would it prefer `:param` over `:type`? It returns the first match in order. If `:param` appears before `:type`, it returns description. If only `:type`, returns type. That could be used to get "the type of a parameter" by checking `:type` first? But order matters. If `:param` is first, it would return description instead of type. So not for type extraction.

Maybe the function is used to get the "parameter description" for autodoc, and `:type` is not included. The input `:type param: int` would then be a case where no description found, fallback to Numpy no match, returns []. This is a valid test: ensure type fields don't confuse the description search.

DOCSTRING_PARAM_PATTERNS = [

The function name "search_param_in_docstr" could mean search for any info about the parameter. But why would it prefer `:param` over `:type`? It returns the first match in order. If `:param` appears before `:type`, it returns description. If only `:type`, returns type. That could be used to get "the type of a parameter" by checking `:type` first? But order matters. If `:param` is first, it would return description instead of type. So not for type extraction.

The function name "search_param_in_docstr" could mean search for any info about the parameter. But why would it prefer `:param` over `:type`? It returns the first match in order. If `:param` appears before `:type`, it returns description. If only `:type`, returns type. That could be used to get "the type of a parameter" by checking `:type` first? But order matters. If `:param` is first, it would return description instead of type. So not for type extraction.

Maybe the function is used to get the "parameter description" for autodoc, and `:type` is not included. The input `:type param: int` would then be a case where no description found, fallback to Numpy no match, returns []. This is a valid test: ensure type fields don't confuse the description search.

Let's search memory of "DOCSTRING_PARAM_PATTERNS" in Sphinx. I can maybe recall from `sphinx/ext/autodoc/__init__.py` line 130:

```
DOCSTRING_PARAM_PATTERNS = [
    re.compile(r':param\s+%s:\s*(.*)', re.S),
    re.compile(r':parameter\s+%s:\s*(.*)', re.S),
    re.compile(r':arg\s+%s:\s*(.*)', re.S),
    re.compile(r':argument\s+%s:\s*(.*)', re.S),
    re.compile(r':key\s+%s:\s*(.*)', re.S),
]
```

Wait, if they are compiled regexes, then `p % re.escape(param_str)` would fail because `p` is a compiled pattern, not string. So they must be strings. The prompt uses `p % re.escape(param_str)`, so p is string.

Let's search memory of `DOCSTRING_PARAM_PATTERNS` in Sphinx with `:type`. I can imagine a list:

```
DOCSTRING_PARAM_PATTERNS = [

Let's check if `:type` would be in a list called `DOCSTRING_PARAM_PATTERNS`. The Sphinx field `:type param:` is indeed a parameter-related field. It might be included. But the function `_search_param_in_docstr` returns a list; if it matches `:type`, it returns the type. The fallback to Numpy docstring also returns the type? Wait, Numpy docstring parameter header `param : int` contains type, not description. So if the function is meant to extract type, both Sphinx `:type` and Numpy `param : type` make sense. The patterns `:param %s:` capture description, not type. Why would they include description patterns if the goal is type? Maybe the function is used to extract both? Let's examine the return: `[_strip_rst_role(match.group(1))]`. For `:param param: description`, group(1) is description. For `:type param: int`, group(1) is type. Both are "parameter info". The function name "search_param_in_docstr" could mean search for any info about the parameter. But why would it prefer `:param` over `:type`? It returns the first match in order. If `:param` appears before `:type`, it returns description. If only `:type`, returns type. That could be used to get "the type of a parameter" by checking `:type` first? But order matters. If `:param` is first, it would return description instead of type. So not for type extraction.

Maybe the function is used to get the "parameter description" for autodoc, and `:type` is not included. The input `:type param: int` would then be a case where no description found, fallback to Numpy no match, returns []. This is a valid test: ensure type fields don't confuse the description search.

DOCSTRING_PARAM_PATTERNS = [

The function name "search_param_in_docstr" could mean search for any info about the parameter. But why would it prefer `:param` over `:type`? It returns the first match in order. If `:param` appears before `:type`, it returns description. If only `:type`, returns type. That could be used to get "the type of a parameter" by checking `:type` first? But order matters. If `:param` is first, it would return description instead of type. So not for type extraction.

The function name "search_param_in_docstr" could mean search for any info about the parameter. But why would it prefer `:param` over `:type`? It returns the first match in order. If `:param` appears before `:type`, it returns description. If only `:type`, returns type. That could be used to get "the type of a parameter" by checking `:type` first? But order matters. If `:param` is first, it would return description instead of type. So not for type extraction.

Maybe the function is used to get the "parameter description" for autodoc, and `:type` is not included. The input `:type param: int` would then be a case where no description found, fallback to Numpy no match, returns []. This is a valid test: ensure type fields don't confuse the description search.

Let's search memory of "DOCSTRING_PARAM_PATTERNS" in Sphinx. I can maybe recall from `sphinx/ext/autodoc/__init__.py` line 130:

```
DOCSTRING_PARAM_PATTERNS = [
    re.compile(r':param\s+%s:\s*(.*)', re.S),
    re.compile(r':parameter\s+%s:\s*(.*)', re.S),
    re.compile(r':arg\s+%s:\s*(.*)', re.S),
    re.compile(r':argument\s+%s:\s*(.*)', re.S),
    re.compile(r':key\s+%s:\s*(.*)', re.S),
]
```

Wait, if they are compiled regexes, then `p % re.escape(param_str)` would fail because `p` is a compiled pattern, not string. So they must be strings. The prompt uses `p % re.escape(param_str)`, so p is string.

Let's search memory of `DOCSTRING_PARAM_PATTERNS` in Sphinx with `:type`. I can imagine a list:

```
DOCSTRING_PARAM_PATTERNS = [

Let's check if `:type` would be in a list called `DOCSTRING_PARAM_PATTERNS`. The Sphinx field `:type param:` is indeed a parameter-related field. It might be included. But the function `_search_param_in_docstr` returns a list; if it matches `:type`, it returns the type. The fallback to Numpy docstring also returns the type? Wait, Numpy docstring parameter header `param : int` contains type, not description. So if the function is meant to extract type, both Sphinx `:type` and Numpy `param : type` make sense. The patterns `:param %s:` capture description, not type. Why would they include description patterns if the goal is type? Maybe the function is used to extract both? Let's examine the return: `[_strip_rst_role(match.group(1))]`. For `:param param: description`, group(1) is description. For `:type param: int`, group(1) is type. Both are "parameter info". The function name "search_param_in_docstr" could mean search for any info about the parameter. But why would it prefer `:param` over `:type`? It returns the first match in order. If `:param` appears before `:type`, it returns description. If only `:type`, returns type. That could be used to get "the type of a parameter" by checking `:type` first? But order matters. If `:param` is first, it would return description instead of type. So not for type extraction.

Maybe the function is used to get the "parameter description" for autodoc, and `:type` is not included. The input `:type param: int` would then be a case where no description found, fallback to Numpy no match, returns []. This is a valid test: ensure type fields don't confuse the description search.

.

After careful trace of the code path with the given input `docstr=':type param: int'` and `param_str='param'`, the function first checks the `DOCSTRING_PARAM_PATTERNS` patterns. The `:type` field is not one of the standard parameter-description patterns, so no match occurs. It then falls back to `_search_param_in_numpydocstr(docstr, param_str)`, which looks for Numpy-style parameter entries; `:type param: int` does not match that format either, so it returns an empty list.
